Helton-Howe Trace, Connes-Chern Character and Quantization
Xiang Tang, Yi Wang, Dechao Zheng

Abstract
We study the Helton-Howe trace and the Connes-Chern character for Toeplitz operators on weighted Bergman spaces via the idea of quantization. We prove a local formula for the large t t -limit of the Connes-Chern character as the weight goes to infinity. And we show that the Helton-Howe trace of Toeplitz operators is independent of the weight t t and obtain a local formula for the Helton-Howe trace for all weighted Bergman spaces using harmonic analysis and quantization. Keywords: Connes-Chern character, Helton-Howe trace, quantization, Toeplitz operator, weighted Bergman space



1
Introduction

Toeplitz extensions are fundamental objects in noncommutative geometry. They are natural examples of finite summable Fredholm modules and define elements in the corresponding
K
-homology group. Trace on Toeplitz operators has been well studied with many interesting results, c.f.
[
53
]
. Since the 70s, trace has been employed to extract geometric information of Toeplitz extension. In particular, Connes
[
14
, Sec. 2., Theorem 5]
used trace on products of semi-commutators to define the Connes-Chern character of finite summable extensions.

For the unit disk
\mathbb{D}
in
\mathbb{C}
, let
L^{2}_{a}(\mathbb{D})
be the Bergman space of
L^{2}
analytic functions on
\mathbb{D}
. Given
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, let
T^{(0)}_{f}
be the Toeplitz operator on
L^{2}_{a}(\mathbb{D})
associated to the symbol
f
. The commutator
[T^{(0)}_{f},T^{(0)}_{g}]:=T^{(0)}_{f}T^{(0)}_{g}-T^{(0)}_{g}T^{(0)}_{f}
for
f,g\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
is a trace class operator. Helton and Howe
[
30
]
discovered an interesting formula for the commutator

\mathrm{Tr}\big([T^{(0)}_{f},T^{(0)}_{g}]\big)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\mathbb{D}}\mathrm{d}f\wedge\mathrm{d}g.

The above result is deeply connected to the Pincus function for a pair of noncommuting selfadjoint operators, c.f.
[
10
,
11
,
42
]
.

For
\mathbb{B}_{n}
, the commutator
[T^{(0)}_{f},T^{(0)}_{g}]
for two Toeplitz operators with smooth symbols
f,g
on
L^{2}_{a}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
is a Schatten-
p
class operator for
p>n
. Suppose
f_{1},...,f_{2n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
. Then the product of the commutators

[T^{(0)}_{f_{1}},T^{(0)}_{f_{2}}]\cdots[T^{(0)}_{f_{2n-1}},T^{(0)}_{2n}]

may not be a trace class operator. Helton and Howe
[
31
,
33
]
made a breakthrough by considering the antisymmetric sum of
T_{f_{1}},...,T_{f_{2n}}
defined by

[T^{(0)}_{f_{1}},...,T^{(0)}_{f_{2n}}]:=\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n}}\operatorname{sgn}(\tau)T^{(0)}_{f_{\tau(1)}}T^{(0)}_{f_{\tau(2)}}...T^{(0)}_{f_{\tau(2n)}},

where
S_{2n}
is the permutations group of
2n
elements and
\operatorname{sgn}
is the sign of the permutation
\tau
. The following is a remarkable generalization of the Helton-Howe trace formula for the commutator of two Toeplitz operators on
L^{2}_{a}(\mathbb{D})
.

Theorem 1.1

(Helton-Howe)
.

On the Bergman space
L^{2}_{a}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
(and the Hardy space
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
), the antisymmetric sum
[T^{(0)}_{f_{1}},...,T^{(0)}_{f_{2n}}]
is a trace class operator, and

\operatorname{Tr}\big([T^{(0)}_{f_{1}},...,T^{(0)}_{f_{2n}}]\big)=\frac{n!}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathrm{d}f_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{2n}.

(1.1)

We observe that by Stoke’s theorem, the above integral only depends on the value of
f_{1},...,f_{2n}
on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}=\partial\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}}
, i.e.

\frac{n!}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathrm{d}f_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{2n}=\frac{n!}{(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}}f_{1}\mathrm{d}f_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{2n}.

The above idea of Schatten-
p
commutators was revolutionized by Connes
[
14
]
into a fundamental concept in noncommutative geometry as
p
-summable Fredholm modules. And the Helton-Howe trace formula in Theorem
1.1
inspired Connes to his ingenious discovery of cyclic cohomology and the Chern character for
p
-summable Fredholm modules. The building block of the Connes-Chern character is the semi-commutator

\sigma_{t}(f,g)=T^{(t)}_{f}T^{(t)}_{g}-T^{(t)}_{fg}.

Modulo constant, the Connes-Chern character for the Toeplitz extension is defined to be

\tau_{t}(f_{0},\cdots,f_{2p-1}):=\operatorname{Tr}\big(\sigma_{t}(f_{0},f_{1})...\sigma_{t}(f_{2p-2},f_{2p-1})\big)-\operatorname{Tr}\big(\sigma_{t}(f_{1},f_{2})...\sigma_{t}(f_{2p-1},f_{0})\big),

(1.2)

for
p>n
. And the Helton-Howe trace, Equation (
1.1
), is the top degree component of the above Connes-Chern character.

Connes observed that the above cocycle in general is not local, i.e. the value of
\tau_{t}(f_{0},\cdots,f_{2p-1})
can not be expressed by the germ of
f_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{2p-1}
on the diagonal in

\underbrace{\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{B}_{n}}_{2p}.

Connes
[
15
,
16
]
improved the Chern character, Equation (
1.2
), by employing the Dixmier trace on the operator ideal
L^{1,\infty}
. In a series of works, Engliš and his coauthors, e.g.
[
22
,
23
,
25
,
26
]
, studied a generalization of the Helton-Howe trace formula by considering the Dixmier trace on the product

[T^{(0)}_{f_{1}},T^{(0)}_{f_{2}}]\cdots[T^{(0)}_{f_{2n-1}},T^{(0)}_{f_{2n}}].

They expressed the Dixmier trace of the above product as an integral of the product of Poisson brackets between
f_{2k-1}
and
f_{2k}
,
k=1,\cdots,n
.

In this article, we take a different approach to study the Connes-Chern character (
1.2
) and the Helton-Howe trace, Theorem
1.1
. Our main idea is to put the Bergman space and Hardy space into the family of weighted Bergman spaces
L^{2}_{a}(\mathbb{B}_{n},\lambda_{t}):=L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
for the measure

\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)=\frac{(n-1)!}{\pi^{n}B(n,t+1)}(1-|z|^{2})^{t}\mathrm{d}m(z),

where
B(n,t+1)
is the Beta function. Let
T^{(t)}_{f}
be the associated Toeplitz operator on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
with symbol
f
. We study the large
t
behavior of the Connes-Chern character and the Helton-Howe trace.

Our first result is about the Helton-Howe trace for
T^{(t)}_{f_{1}},\ldots,T^{(t)}_{f_{2n}}
for functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{2n}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
, which generalizes Theorem
1.1
to all weighted Bergman spaces.

Theorem 1.2
.

(Theorem
8.1
)
Suppose
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{2n}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
and
t\geq-1
.

1.

[T^{(t)}_{f_{1}},T^{(t)}_{f_{2}},\ldots,T^{(t)}_{f_{2n}}]
is in the trace class
\mathcal{S}^{1}
.

2.

\mathrm{Tr}[T^{(t)}_{f_{1}},T^{(t)}_{f_{2}},\ldots,T^{(t)}_{f_{2n}}]=\frac{n!}{(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathrm{d}f_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{2n},

(1.3)

which is independent of
t
.

Thanks to the use of pseudodifferential calculus and its generalization in the proof, Helton-Howe’s original theorem needs to assume that that the Toeplitz operators to have smooth symbols. In this paper, we develop a new approach to study the trace formula using harmonic analysis. As a result, we obtain an improvement of the Helton-Howe trace formula for Toeplitz operators with
\mathscr{C}^{2}
symbols.

Our second result is about the Connes-Chern character
\tau_{t}(f_{0},\ldots,f_{2p-1})
for
f_{0},\ldots,f_{2p-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
. Different from the Helton-Howe trace,
\tau_{t}
vanishes as
t
goes to
\infty
. In the following theorem, we identify the leading term of
\tau_{t}
as
t\to\infty
.

Theorem 1.3
.

(Theorem
8.6
)
Suppose
p\geq n+1
is an integer and
f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{2p-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
. Then

\begin{split}&\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{p-n}\tau_{t}(f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{2p-1})\\
=&\frac{n^{p}}{\pi^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\bigg(\prod_{j=0}^{p-1}C_{1}(f_{2j},f_{2j+1})(z)-\prod_{j=0}^{p-1}C_{1}(f_{2j+1},f_{2j+2})(z)\bigg)\frac{\mathrm{d}m(z)}{(1-|z|^{2})^{n+1}},\end{split}

where
C_{1}(f,g)
is defined as follows,

C_{1}(f,g)(z)=-\frac{1}{n}(1-|z|^{2})\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}f(z)\bar{\partial}_{i}g(z)-Rf(z)\bar{R}g(z)\bigg],\ R=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\partial_{z_{i}},\ \overline{R}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{z}_{i}\overline{\partial}_{z_{i}}.

(1.4)

Our approach to the above two main theorems is heavily influenced by the idea of quantization,
[
6
,
8
,
9
,
12
,
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
]
. Geometrically the defining function
\psi=1-|z|^{2}
on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
defines the Bergman metric in the following way.

\begin{split}\omega:=&i\frac{-\psi\partial\bar{\partial}\psi+\partial\psi\wedge\bar{\partial}\psi}{\psi^{2}}\\
=&i\frac{(1-|z|^{2})\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial z_{j}\wedge\bar{\partial}\bar{z}_{j}+(\sum_{j}\bar{z}_{j}\partial z_{j})\wedge(\sum_{j^{\prime}}z_{j^{\prime}}\bar{\partial}\bar{z}_{j^{\prime}})}{(1-|z|^{2})^{2}}\end{split}

defines a symplectic form on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
, c.f.
[
37
, Prop. 2.6]
. The Toeplitz operator
T^{(t)}_{f}
gives a quantization of the symplectic form
i\omega
, e.g.
[
20
, Theorem 3]
, satisfying

||T^{(t)}_{f}T^{(t)}_{g}-\sum_{j=0}^{k}t^{-j}T^{(t)}_{C_{j}(f,g)}||=O(t^{-k-1}),\ t\to\infty,

(1.5)

where
C_{j}
is a bilinear operator on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
and
C_{1}
is defined in Equation (
1.4
). The asymptotic expansion formula (
1.5
) provides the key tool to study the semi-commutator,

\sigma_{t}(f,g)=T^{(t)}_{f}T^{(t)}_{g}-T^{(t)}_{fg}.

The asymptotic expansion formula (
1.5
) in the literature, e.g.
[
20
]
, was well studied for estimates on the operator norm. Estimates about the Schatten-
p
norm in the expansion (
1.5
) are needed in our applications to the tracial property in Theorem
1.2
and
1.3
. We prove these estimates in Theorem
6.1
. As we need to study Toeplitz operators with
\mathscr{C}^{2}
symbols in Theorem
1.2
and
1.3
and an estimate on Schatten-
p
norm in Theorem
6.1
, we need a new method to develop the asymptotic estimate in Theorem
6.1
different from the classical method via pseudodifferential/Toeplitz operator calculus
[
7
,
8
,
18
,
20
,
22
,
23
,
25
,
26
,
35
]
, which requires to work with smooth symbols. Our main tool comes from integration formulas developed in Section 4 of
[
47
]
. Theorem
1.3
follows from the Schatten-
p
estimate of the semi-commutator
\sigma_{t}(f,g)
. As a byproduct, our method also provides an explicit algorithm to compute the bilinear differential operator
C_{j}
in the asymptotic expansion (
1.5
), which is in general hard to compute.

A crucial fact used in our estimate is the different behavior of the quantization in complex normal and complex tangential directions (see Remark
6.4
and Corollaries
6.7
,
6.8
). Roughly speaking, the expansion of the integral kernel in the complex tangential direction creates improvement of
\frac{1}{2}
in the Schatten-
p
membership, whereas the expansion of the integral kernel in the complex normal direction creates improvement of
1
in the Schatten-
p
membership. Essentially, this allows us to reduce our estimates to the complex tangential direction. In contrast, in pseudodifferential calculous, Helton and Howe
[
31
]
considered symbol functions for order 0 operators that are constant along the radial direction far away from the zero section of the cotangent bundle, which simplifies the corresponding estimates. And the difference between tangential and radial estimates suggests a deep link of our study with the Heisenberg calculus for contact manifolds, e.g.
[
5
,
32
,
43
,
48
]
.

Instead of a direct computation as in
[
31
]
, we prove Theorem
1.2
in two steps. Suppose
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{2n}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
and
t\geq-1
.

1.

\mathrm{Tr}[T^{(t)}_{f_{1}},T^{(t)}_{f_{2}},\ldots,T^{(t)}_{f_{2n}}]=\mathrm{Tr}[T^{(t+1)}_{f_{1}},T^{(t+1)}_{f_{2}},\ldots,T^{(t+1)}_{f_{2n}}].

(1.6)

2.

\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathrm{Tr}[T^{(s)}_{f_{1}},\ldots,T^{(s)}_{f_{2n}}]=\frac{n!}{(2\pi i)^{n}}\int\mathrm{d}f_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{2n}.

(1.7)

Equation (
1.6
) is proved using the decomposition formula
T^{(t)}_{f_{i}}=A_{i}+B_{i}
in Section
5
, Lemmas
5.4
and
5.6
. Here the operator
A_{i}=T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{i}}
is the restriction of
T^{(t+1)}_{f_{i}}
on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
. We observe that
A_{i}
is the “principal part” of
T^{(t)}_{f_{i}}
for
f_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
. In Section
5
we show that

\mathrm{Tr}\big\{[T^{(t)}_{f_{1}},T^{(t)}_{f_{2}},\ldots,T^{(t)}_{f_{2n}}]-[A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n}]\big\}=0.

(1.8)

We point out that the “minor part”
B_{i}
does not live in Schatten class
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for
p
small enough. This fact prevents us from proving (
1.8
) only using operator-theoretic tools. In Section
5
, we develop Hypotheses A which handles the operator-theoretic part of the proof of (
1.8
), and Hypotheses B, where the rest is handled. As
[T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{1}},\ldots,T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{2n}}]
is the restriction of
[T^{(t+1)}_{f_{1}},T^{(t+1)}_{f_{2}},\ldots,T^{(t+1)}_{f_{2n}}]
on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
, Lemma
2.4
gives

\mathrm{Tr}[T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{1}},\ldots,T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{2n}}]=\mathrm{Tr}[T^{(t+1)}_{f_{1}},\ldots,T^{(t+1)}_{f_{2n}}].

(1.9)

We obtain Equation (
1.6
) by combining Equations (
1.8
) and (
1.9
).

Equation (
1.7
) is proved by improving Theorem
1.3
. In Theorem
1.3
,
p
is assumed to be greater than or equal to
n+1
, while there are only
2n
functions in Equation (
1.7
). We observe that in general the product
\sigma_{t}(f_{1},f_{2})\ldots\sigma_{t}(f_{2n-1},f_{2n})
is not a trace class operator. This leads us to introduce even and odd partial antisymmetrizations in Section
7
. For
f_{1},...,f_{n},g_{1},...,g_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
, define

[f_{1},g_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{n}]^{\mathrm{odd}}_{t}=\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)\sigma_{t}(f_{\tau(1)},g_{1})\ldots\sigma_{t}(f_{\tau(n)},g_{n}),

and

[f_{1},g_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{n}]^{\mathrm{even}}_{t}=\sum_{\tau\in S_{n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)\sigma_{t}(f_{1},g_{\tau(1)})\ldots\sigma_{t}(f_{n},g_{\tau(n)}).

Using the asymptotic expansion of semi-commutator in Section
6
, we improve Theorem
1.3
to the even and odd partial antisymmetrizations.

Theorem 1.4
.

(Theorem
7.3
)
Suppose
t\geq-1
and
f_{1},g_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
.
Then the partial antisymmetrizations
[f_{1},g_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{n}]_{t}^{\mathrm{odd}}
and
[f_{1},g_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{n}]_{t}^{\mathrm{even}}
are in the trace class. Moreover,

\begin{split}\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{Tr}[f_{1},g_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{n}]_{t}^{\mathrm{odd}}=&\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{Tr}[f_{1},g_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{n}]_{t}^{\mathrm{even}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\partial f_{1}\wedge\bar{\partial}g_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\partial f_{n}\wedge\bar{\partial}g_{n}.\end{split}

As a corollary of Theorem
1.4
, we obtain Equation (
1.7
) by further antisymmetrization of the even (odd) antisymmetric sums.

Our solution to the generalized Helton-Howe trace formula through quantization is closely related to the method developed in
[
9
]
to a solution to the Atiyah-Weinstein conjecture for quantized contact transform. Such a similarity suggests that our developments can be generalized to strongly pseudoconvex domains, egg domains, Fock spaces, submodules and their quotient modules of
L^{2}_{a,t}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
, e.g.
[
27
]
, and the Dury-Arveson spaces. More generally, we hope that our study will shed a light on constructing new cyclic cocycles beyond the Helton-Howe traces, which could have applications in noncommutative geometry.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we recall the definitions of weighted Bergman spaces and Hardy spaces together with their basic properties and properties of Schatten
p
-class operators. Some tools developed in
[
47
]
are also reviewed. In Section
3
, we develop criteria for integral operators to be bounded between different weighted spaces
L^{2}_{a,t}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
. We develop some useful estimate for integral operators to belong to Schatten-
p
class in Section
4
. In Section
5
, we prove Equation (
1.8
). We develop the asymptotic expansion formula, Theorem
1.4
, in Section
6
. We introduce the even and odd antisymmetrization and prove Theorem
7.3
and Equation (
1.7
) in Section
7
. The two main theorems, Theorem
1.2
and
1.3
, are proved in Section
8
. And a proof of the generalized Bochner-Martinelli formula is included in the appendix.

We would like to thank Mohammad Jabbari, Richard Rochberg, and Jingbo Xia for inspiring discussions. Tang is partially supported by NSF Grants DMS 1800666, 1952551; Wang is partially supported by NSF Grant 2101370.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we recall some basic definitions and properties about weighted Bergman spaces and Schatten-
p
class operators.

2.1
Spaces on
\mathbb{B}_{n}

Let
\mathbb{B}_{n}
be the open unit ball of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\mathbb{S}_{n}=\partial\mathbb{B}_{n}
the unit sphere. Let
m
be the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
and
\sigma
be the surface measure on
\mathbb{S}_{n}
. Denote
\sigma_{2n-1}=\sigma(\mathbb{S}_{n})=\frac{2\pi^{n}}{(n-1)!}
.

Hardy Space:
The Hardy space
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
is the Hilbert space of holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
with the norm

\|f\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})}^{2}=\sup_{0<r<1}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}|f(rz)|^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma(z)}{\sigma_{2n-1}}.

Equivalently,
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
is the closure of analytic polynomials in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n}):=L^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n},\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma}{\sigma_{2n-1}})
. The Hardy space is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
and the reproducing kernel is

K_{w}(z)=\frac{1}{(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{n}},\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

For any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
, the Toeplitz operator on
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
with symbol
f
is defined to be the compression

T_{f}=PM_{f}|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})},

where
M_{f}
is the pointwise multiplication on
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
, and
P
is the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
onto
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
.
Using the reproducing kernel, we can write
T_{f}
as an integral operator. For
h\in H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
,

T_{f}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}f(w)h(w)K_{w}(z)\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma(z)}{\sigma_{2n-1}},\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

Our discussion will also involve Hankel operators. The Hankel operator with symbol
f
is
H_{f}=(I-P)M_{f}P
from
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
.

Weighted Bergman Spaces:
For
t>-1
, define the probability measure on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
:

\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)=\frac{(n-1)!}{\pi^{n}B(n,t+1)}(1-|z|^{2})^{t}\mathrm{d}m(z).

Here
B(n,t+1)
is the Beta function. The weighted Bergman space
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
is the subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n},\lambda_{t})
consisting of holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
. The reproducing kernel of
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
is

K^{(t)}_{w}(z)=\frac{1}{(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{n+1+t}},\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

For any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
, the Toeplitz operator
T^{(t)}_{f}
is the compression

T^{(t)}_{f}=P^{(t)}M^{(t)}_{f}|_{L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})},

where
P^{(t)}
is the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n},\lambda_{t})
onto
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
, and
M^{(t)}_{f}
is the multiplication operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n},\lambda_{t})
. The Hankel operator with symbol
f
is

H^{(t)}_{f}=(I-P^{(t)})M_{f}^{(t)}P^{(t)}.

Using the reproducing kernels, we can write
T^{(t)}_{f},H^{(t)}_{f}
as integral operators. For
h\in L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
, we have the following expressions,

T^{(t)}_{f}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}f(w)h(w)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w),\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{B}_{n},

H^{(t)}_{f}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\big(f(z)-f(w)\big)h(w)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w),\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

It is sometimes understood that the Hardy space is the weighted Bergman space at
t=-1
. This is partially justified by the form of its reproducing kernel.

An important tool on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
is the Möbius transform.

Definition 2.1
.

For
z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}
,
z\neq 0
, the Möbius transform
\varphi_{z}
is the biholomorphic mapping on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
defined as follows.

\varphi_{z}(w)=\frac{z-P_{z}(w)-(1-|z|^{2})^{1/2}Q_{z}(w)}{1-\langle w,z\rangle},\quad\forall w\in\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}}.

Here
P_{z}
and
Q_{z}
denote the orthogonal projection from
\mathbb{C}^{n}
onto
\mathbb{C}z
and
z^{\perp}
, respectively.
Define

\varphi_{0}(w)=-w,\quad\forall w\in\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}}.

It is well-known that
\varphi_{z}
is an automorphism of
\mathbb{B}_{n}
satisfying
\varphi_{z}\circ\varphi_{z}=Id
. Also, the two variable function
\rho(z,w):=|\varphi_{z}(w)|=|\varphi_{w}(z)|
defines a metric on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
. Moreover,
\beta(z,w):=\tanh^{-1}\rho(z,w)
coincides with the Bergman metric on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
.

We list some lemmas that serve as basic tools on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
. Most of the following can be found in
[
44
,
52
]
. Some are proved in our other paper
[
47
]
.

For non-negative values
A,B
, by
A\lesssim B
we mean that there is a constant
C
such that
A\leq CB
. Sometimes, to emphasize that the constant
C
depends on some parameter
a
, we write
A\lesssim_{a}B
. The notations
\gtrsim,\gtrsim_{a},\approx,\approx_{a}
are defined similarly.

Lemma 2.2
.

(
[
47
, Lemma 2.2]
)
Suppose
z,w,\zeta\in\mathbb{B}_{n}
.

(1)

\frac{1}{1-\langle\varphi_{\zeta}(z),\varphi_{\zeta}(w)\rangle}=\frac{(1-\langle z,\zeta\rangle)(1-\langle\zeta,w\rangle)}{(1-|\zeta|^{2})(1-\langle z,w\rangle)}
.

(2)

1-|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2}=\frac{(1-|z|^{2})(1-|w|^{2})}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{2}}
.

(3)

For any
R>0
there exists
C>1
such that whenever
\beta(z,w)<R
,

\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{1-|w|^{2}}\leq C,\quad\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{|1-\langle z,\zeta\rangle|}{|1-\langle w,\zeta\rangle|}\leq C.

(4)

The real Jacobian of
\varphi_{z}
is
\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{n+1}}{|1-\langle z,\cdot\rangle|^{2n+2}}
on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
and
\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{n}}{|1-\langle z,\cdot\rangle|^{2n}}
on
\mathbb{S}_{n}
.

(5)

For
z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}
,

z-\varphi_{z}(w)=\frac{(1-|z|^{2})P_{z}(w)+(1-|z|^{2})^{1/2}Q_{z}(w)}{1-\langle w,z\rangle}:=\frac{A_{z}w}{1-\langle w,z\rangle},

where
A_{z}=[a_{z}^{ij}]
is an
n\times n
matrix depending on
z
, and
w
is viewed as a column vector.

(6)

There exists
C>0
such that for any
z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}
,
z\neq 0
,

|z-P_{z}(w)|\leq|\varphi_{z}(w)||1-\langle z,w\rangle|,\quad|Q_{z}(w)|\leq C|\varphi_{z}(w)||1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{1/2},

(2.1)

and

|z-w|\leq C|\varphi_{z}(w)||1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{1/2}.

(2.2)

In contrast, if
n=1
, then
|z-w|=|\varphi_{z}(w)||1-z\bar{w}|
.

(7)

1-|z|^{2}\leq 2|1-\langle z,w\rangle|
.

Lemma 2.3
.

(
[
47
, Lemma 2.4]
)

(1)

Suppose
t>-1
,
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Then there exists
C>0
such that for any
z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}
,

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{t}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t+c}}\mathrm{d}m(w)\leq\begin{cases}C(1-|z|^{2})^{-c},&c>0,\\
C\ln\frac{1}{1-|z|^{2}},&c=0,\\
C,&c<0,\end{cases}

(2.3)

\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}\frac{1}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+c}}\mathrm{d}\sigma(w)\leq\begin{cases}C(1-|z|^{2})^{-c},&c>0,\\
C\ln\frac{1}{1-|z|^{2}},&c=0,\\
C,&c<0.\end{cases}

(2.4)

(2)

Suppose
t>-1
,
a,b,c>0
,
a\geq c,b\geq c
, and
a+b<n+1+t+c
. Then there exists
C>0
such that for any
z,\xi\in\mathbb{B}_{n}
,

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{t}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{a}|1-\langle w,\xi\rangle|^{b}}\mathrm{d}m(w)\leq C\frac{1}{|1-\langle z,\xi\rangle|^{c}}.

(2.5)

(3)

Suppose
\phi:(0,1)\to[0,\infty)
is measurable. Suppose
a>-n
,
b\in\mathbb{R}
, and

\phi(s)\lesssim s^{a}(1-s)^{b},\quad s\in(0,1).

Then for any
t>-1-b
,
c>-b
there exists
C>0
such that for any
z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}
,

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{t}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t+c}}\mathrm{d}m(w)\leq C(1-|z|^{2})^{-c}.

(2.6)

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose
s>t\geq-1
and
T
is a bounded operator on
L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
. Suppose
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
is invariant under
T
. Denote
\hat{T}
its restriction to
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
. By the closed graph theorem,
\hat{T}
is bounded on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
. Assume that
T\in\mathcal{S}^{1}\big(L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\big)
and
\hat{T}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}\big(L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\big)
. Then

\mathrm{Tr}T=\mathrm{Tr}\hat{T}.

Proof.

The set
\{z^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}
forms an orthogonal basis of both
L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
and
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
. For
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
, we write

Tz^{\alpha}=\hat{T}z^{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}a_{\alpha,\beta}z^{\beta}.

Compute the traces of
T
and
\hat{T}
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}T=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}\frac{\langle Tz^{\alpha},z^{\alpha}\rangle_{L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})}}{\|z^{\alpha}\|_{L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}\frac{a_{\alpha,\alpha}\langle z^{\alpha},z^{\alpha}\rangle_{L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})}}{\|z^{\alpha}\|^{2}_{L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}a_{\alpha,\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}\frac{\langle\hat{T}z^{\alpha},z^{\alpha}\rangle_{L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})}}{\|z^{\alpha}\|_{L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})}^{2}}=\mathrm{Tr}\hat{T}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
2.4
.
∎

2.2
Schatten Class Operators

For
p>0
, a bounded operator
T
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is said to be in the Schatten-
p
class
\mathcal{S}^{p}
if
|T|^{p}
belongs to the trace class. The Schatten-
p
class operators
\mathcal{S}^{p}
are analogues of
l^{p}
spaces in the operator-theoretic setting. Conventionally,
\mathcal{S}^{\infty}
denotes the space of compact operators. The following two lemmas will be used constantly.

Lemma 2.5
.

(
[
46
, Theorem 2.8]
)
Suppose
A
,
B
are bounded operators on a Hilbert space, and
1\leq p,q,r\leq\infty
,
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{r}
. If
A\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
and
B\in\mathcal{S}^{q}
, then

AB\in\mathcal{S}^{r},\quad\text{ and }\quad\|AB\|_{\mathcal{S}^{r}}\leq\|A\|_{\mathcal{S}^{p}}\|B\|_{\mathcal{S}^{q}}.

Lemma 2.6
.

(
[
46
, Corollary 3.8]
)
Suppose
A,B
are bounded operators on a Hilbert space. If both
AB
and
BA
are in the trace class then

\mathrm{Tr}[A,B]=0.

Remark 2.7
.

Suppose
n>1
and
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
are bounded operators on a Hilbert space such that

X_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\quad\forall p>n,

and there exists
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
X_{j}
is in
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for some
p<n
. Then we can choose
p_{1},\ldots,p_{j-1},p_{j+1},\ldots,p_{n}>n
and
1\leq p_{j}<n
such that

X_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{p_{i}},i=1,\ldots,n,\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}+\ldots+\frac{1}{p_{n}}=1.

By an inductive application of Lemma
2.5
we have

X_{1}X_{2}\ldots X_{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}.

Moreover, by Lemma
2.6
, for any
k=1,\ldots,n
,

\mathrm{Tr}X_{1}X_{2}\ldots X_{n}=\mathrm{Tr}X_{k}X_{k+1}\ldots X_{n}X_{1}\ldots X_{k-1}.

This will be used repeatedly in this paper.

2.3
Integration by Parts

Some integral formulas developed in
[
47
]
will be used in our proofs. We give a brief review here. Let us start with some auxiliary functions and operations.

Recall that by Lemma
2.2
(5), for
z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}
,

(1-|z|^{2})P_{z}(w)+(1-|z|^{2})^{1/2}Q_{z}(w)=(1-\langle w,z\rangle)(z-\varphi_{z}(w))=A_{z}w,

where
A_{z}
is an
n\times n
matrix depending on
z
, and
w
is treated as a column vector.

Definition 2.8
.

For multi-indices
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
and
z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}
, define

d_{\alpha,\beta}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}\big(A_{z}\zeta\big)^{\alpha}\overline{\big(A_{z}\zeta\big)}^{\beta}\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma(\zeta)}{\sigma_{2n-1}}.

In particular,
d_{0,0}=1
, and

d_{\alpha,\beta}(z)=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}(1-|z|^{2})^{\alpha_{1}+|\alpha|}\frac{(n-1)!\alpha!}{(n-1+|\alpha|)!},\quad\text{if }z=(z_{1},0,\ldots,0).

(2.7)

For multi-indices
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, denote

I^{\alpha,\beta}(\zeta)=\zeta^{\alpha}\bar{\zeta}^{\beta}.

Definition 2.9
.

For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, denote

\phi_{t}(s)=(1-s)^{t}.

Suppose
\phi:(0,1)\to[0,\infty)
is a measurable function. For a positive integer
m
and any
t>-1
, define the operations on
\phi

\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{m}\phi(s)=\int_{s}^{1}r^{m-1}\phi(r)(1-r)^{t}\mathrm{d}r\in[0,\infty],

(2.8)

and

\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{m}\phi(s)=\frac{1}{s^{m}\phi_{t+1}(s)}\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{m}\phi(s)=\frac{\int_{s}^{1}r^{m-1}\phi(r)(1-r)^{t}\mathrm{d}r}{s^{m}(1-s)^{t+1}}\in[0,\infty].

(2.9)

For any
t>-1
, inductively define the functions

\Phi_{n,0}^{(t)}\equiv 1,\quad\Phi_{n,k+1}^{(t)}=M_{\phi_{1}}\big(\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n+k}\big)^{2}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}.

Equivalently,

\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}=M_{\phi_{1}}\big(\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n+k-1}\big)^{2}\ldots M_{\phi_{1}}\big(\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n}\big)^{2}1.

(2.10)

Lemma 2.10
.

(
[
47
, Lemma 4.2]
)
Suppose
t>-1
,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
. Suppose
\phi:(0,1)\to[0,\infty)
is measurable and
v\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
. Then the following hold.

1.

If
|\alpha|\geq|\beta|
and all integrals converge absolutely, then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})I^{\alpha,\beta}(z-w)v(w)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w)

(2.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{d_{\alpha,\beta}(z)}{B(n,t+1)}\cdot\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+|\beta|}\phi(0)v(z)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{|\beta|+n}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})I^{\alpha,\beta+e_{j}}(z-w)S_{j}(w)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w),&\\
\hskip 284.52756ptv(z)\neq 0,\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+|\beta|}\phi(0)<\infty,&\\
&\\
-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{|\beta|+n}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})I^{\alpha,\beta+e_{j}}(z-w)S_{j}(w)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w),&\\
\hskip 284.52756ptv(z)=0,\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+|\beta|}\phi(0)\leq\infty,&\end{cases}

where

S_{j}(w,z)=\frac{(1-|w|^{2})\bar{\partial}_{w_{j}}\big[(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{|\beta|}v(w)\big]}{(1-\langle w,z\rangle)(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{|\beta|}}.

2.

If
|\alpha|\leq|\beta|
and all integrals converge absolutely, then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})I^{\alpha,\beta}(z-w)v(z)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)

(2.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{d_{\alpha,\beta}(w)}{B(n,t+1)}\cdot\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+|\alpha|}\phi(0)v(w)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{|\alpha|+n}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})I^{\alpha+e_{i},\beta}(z-w)\widetilde{S}_{i}(z)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z),&\\
\hskip 284.52756ptv(w)\neq 0,\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+|\alpha|}\phi(0)<\infty,&\\
&\\
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{|\alpha|+n}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})I^{\alpha+e_{i},\beta}(z-w)\widetilde{S}_{i}(z)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z),&\\
\hskip 284.52756ptv(w)=0,\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+|\alpha|}\phi(0)\leq\infty,&\end{cases}

where

\widetilde{S}_{i}(z,w)=\frac{(1-|z|^{2})\partial_{z_{i}}\big[(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{|\alpha|}v(z)\big]}{(1-\langle w,z\rangle)(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{|\alpha|}}.

A particular case is to take
\alpha=\beta=0
,
z=0
,
\phi=1
in (
2.11
), which gives the following. The estimates (2) and (3) are straight forward to verify.

Lemma 2.11
.

For any
t>-1
the following hold.

(1)

For any
v\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
,

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}v(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)=v(0)+\frac{t+1}{n+t+1}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n}1(|z|^{2})\bar{R}v(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t+1}(z).

(2.13)

(2)

\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n}1(s)\approx s^{-n}
in a neighborhood of
0
, and
\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n}1(s)=\frac{1}{t+1}
.

(3)

|\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n}1(s)-\frac{1}{t+1}|\lesssim 1-s
for
s
in a neighborhood of
1
.

Lemma 2.12
.

(
[
47
, Lemma 4.6]
)
Suppose
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
, and
v\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
. Then the following hold.

1.

If
|\alpha|\geq|\beta|
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}I^{\alpha,\beta}(z-w)v(w)K_{w}(z)\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma(w)}{\sigma_{2n-1}}

(2.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\alpha,\beta}(z)v(z)-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{-2|\beta|-2n}I^{\alpha,\beta+e_{j}}(z-w)\frac{\bar{\partial}_{j}\big[(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{|\beta|}v(w)\big]}{(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{|\beta|}(1-\langle w,z\rangle)}K_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{0}(w).

2.

If
|\alpha|\leq|\beta|
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}I^{\alpha,\beta}(z-w)v(z)K_{w}(z)\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma(z)}{\sigma_{2n-1}}

(2.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\beta,\alpha}(w)v(w)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{-2|\alpha|-2n}I^{\alpha+e_{i},\beta}(z-w)\frac{\partial_{i}\big[(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{|\alpha|}v(z)\big]}{(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{|\alpha|}(1-\langle w,z\rangle)}K_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{0}(z).

Lemma 2.13
.

(
[
47
, Lemma 4.3]
)
Suppose
k
is a non-negative integer and
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
is a finite set of multi-indices with
|\alpha|=|\beta|=k
for every
(\alpha,\beta)\in\Gamma
. Suppose for some
\epsilon>-1-t
,
\{F_{\alpha,\beta}\}_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Gamma}\subset\mathscr{C}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{B}_{n})
and

\bigg|\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Gamma}I^{\alpha,\beta}(z-w)F_{\alpha,\beta}(z,w)\bigg|\lesssim|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2k}|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{2k+\epsilon},

(2.16)

\bigg|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Gamma}I^{\alpha,\beta+e_{j}}(z-w)\bar{\partial}_{w_{j}}F_{\alpha,\beta}(z,w)\bigg|\lesssim|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2k+1}|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{2k+\epsilon}.

(2.17)

Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}^{2}}\Phi^{(t)}_{n,k}(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})\frac{\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Gamma}I^{\alpha,\beta}(z-w)F_{\alpha,\beta}(z,w)}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{2k}}K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+k}\Phi^{(t)}_{n,k}(0)}{B(n,t+1)}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}(1-|z|^{2})^{-2k}\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Gamma}d_{\alpha,\beta}(z)F_{\alpha,\beta}(z,z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)

(2.18)

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}^{2}}\Phi^{(t)}_{n,k+1}(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})\frac{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Gamma}I^{\alpha+e_{i},\beta+e_{j}}(z-w)D_{i,j}F_{\alpha,\beta}(z,w)}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{2(k+1)}}K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w).

Here
D_{i,j}
denotes the operation

D_{i,j}=(1-\langle z,w\rangle)^{2}\partial_{z_{i}}\bar{\partial}_{w_{j}}.

Lemma 2.14
.

Suppose
k
is a nonnegative integer. Then there exist
C>c>0
such that for
t
large enough,

ct^{-n-k}\leq\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+k}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(0)\leq Ct^{-n-k},

(2.19)

and

\int_{0}^{1}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(s)s^{n+k-1}(1-s)^{\frac{t}{4}}\mathrm{d}s\leq Ct^{-n-k}.

(2.20)

Proof.

The case when
k=0
is simply a consequence of
[
47
, Equation (8.9)]
. We assume
k>0
.

From the Sterling’s asymptotic formula (cf.
[
2
, Theorem 1.4.1]
) and the well-known identity
B(x,y)=\frac{\Gamma(x)\Gamma(y)}{\Gamma(x+y)}
it follows that for fixed
x>-1
,

B(x,y)\approx_{x}y^{-x}

for large
y
.
By
[
47
, Lemma 8.4]
, we compute
\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+k}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(0)
as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+k}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+k}M_{\phi_{1}}\big(\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n+k-1}\big)^{2}\ldots M_{\phi_{1}}\big(\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n}\big)^{2}1(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}=0}^{\infty}\frac{B(n+k+j_{1}+\ldots+j_{k},t+1)}{1\cdot(1+j_{1})(2+j_{1})(2+j_{1}+j_{2})\ldots(k+j_{1}+\ldots+j_{k-1})(k+j_{1}+\ldots+j_{k})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq s_{1}\leq\ldots\leq s_{k}<\infty}\frac{B(n+k+s_{k},t+1)}{(1+s_{1})(2+s_{1})\ldots(k-1+s_{k-1})(k+s_{k-1})(k+s_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{k}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{k-1}=0}^{\infty}\frac{B(n+k+s_{k},t+1)}{(1+s_{1})(2+s_{1})\ldots(k-1+s_{k-1})(k+s_{k-1})(k+s_{k})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{k}=0}^{\infty}\frac{B(n+k+s_{k},t+1)}{(k-1)!(k+s_{k})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(k-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\big(\sum_{s_{k}=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{n-1+(k+s_{k})}}{k+s_{k}}\big)(1-x)^{t}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(k-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\big(\sum_{s_{k}=0}^{\infty}x^{n-1}\bigg(\ln\frac{1}{1-x}\bigg)(1-x)^{t}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}x^{n-1}(1-x)^{t-1}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B(n,t)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle t^{-n-k},

when
t
is large. The other inequality
\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+k}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(0)\gtrsim t^{-n-k}
is also obvious from the equation above. This proves (
2.19
).

By the expansion

\frac{1}{(1-s)^{\frac{3t}{4}}}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4}+j)}{j!\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4})}s^{j},

we directly compute the following integral,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(s)s^{n+k-1}(1-s)^{\frac{t}{4}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4}+j)}{j!\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4})}\int_{0}^{1}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(s)s^{n+k+j-1}(1-s)^{t}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{0}=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4}+j_{0})}{j_{0}!\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4})}\mathcal{F}^{(t)}_{n+k+j_{0}}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(0).

Similarly as the proof of (
2.19
), assuming
t
is large enough, we estimate the above term as follows,

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq s_{0}\leq s_{k}<\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4}+s_{0})B(n+k+s_{k},t+1)}{(s_{0}+1)!\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4})(k-1)!(k+s_{k})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\sum_{b=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4}+a)B(n+k+a+b,t+1)}{(a+1)!\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4})(k-1)!(k+a+b)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4}+a)}{(a+1)!\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4})(k-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{b=0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{b+1}}{k+a+b}s^{n+k+a-2}(1-s)^{t}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4}+a)}{(a+1)!\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4})(k-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{b=0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{b+1}}{b+1}s^{n+k+a-2}(1-s)^{t}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4}+a)}{(a+1)!\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4})(k-1)!}\int_{0}^{1}s^{n+k+a-2}(1-s)^{t}\ln\frac{1}{1-s}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4}+a)}{(a+1)!\Gamma(\frac{3t}{4})(k-1)!}B(n+k+a,t)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}s^{n+k-1}(1-s)^{t-1-\frac{3t}{4}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B(n+k,\frac{t}{4})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle t^{-n-k}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
2.14
.
∎

3
Operators Between Weighted Spaces

For our proofs in this paper it is important to get criteria for integral operators to be bounded between different weighted spaces. In this section, we obtain such criteria. Most of the lemmas established in this section follow from standard techniques from, for example,
[
44
,
52
]
. One thing less standard is that our integral kernels may involve functions of
|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2}
. These functions come from applying the integration by parts formulas in Subsection
2.3
on integral formulas of Toeplitz operators.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose
t>-1,s>-1
,
a>-n
,
b\geq 0
, and
c<t+1+b-\frac{s+1}{2}
. Suppose
F(z,w)
is measurable on
\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{B}_{n}
, and

|F(z,w)|\leq\frac{|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2a}(1-|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})^{b}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}},\quad\forall z,w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

Then the integral operator

Th(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)F(z,w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w)

is bounded from
L^{2}(\lambda_{s+2c})
to
L^{2}(\lambda_{s})
.

Proof.

By assumption, we can take
x\in\mathbb{R}
so that

\max\{-1-t-b,-c-s-1-b\}<x<\min\{b-c,t+b-2c-s\}.

Then we have the following inequalities,

t+x>-1-b,\quad-x-c>-b,\quad x+c+s>-1-b,\quad t-x-2c-s>-b.

Take
p(w)=(1-|w|^{2})^{x}
and
q(z)=(1-|z|^{2})^{x+c}
. The integral kernel of
T
as an operator from
L^{2}(\lambda_{s+2c})
to
L^{2}(\lambda_{s})
is

\frac{B(n,s+2c+1)}{B(n,t+1)}F(z,w)(1-|w|^{2})^{t-s-2c}.

Then by the inequalities above and Lemma
2.3
(3), we have the following estimates of integrals,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|F(z,w)|(1-|w|^{2})^{t-s-2c}p(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{s+2c}(w)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2a}(1-|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})^{b}\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{t+x}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}}\mathrm{d}m(w)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1-|z|^{2})^{x+c}=q(z),

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|F(z,w)|(1-|w|^{2})^{t-s-2c}q(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{s}(z)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2a}(1-|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})^{b}\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{t-s-2c}(1-|z|^{2})^{x+c+s}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}}\mathrm{d}m(z)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1-|w|^{2})^{x}=p(w).

The conclusion follows from Schur’s test (cf.
[
53
, Theorem 3.6]
). This completes the proof of Lemma
3.1
.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose
t>-1
and
0<d<c<t+1
. Suppose
F(z,w)
is piecewise continuous on
\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{B}_{n}
, and

|F(z,w)|\leq\frac{1}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}},\quad\forall z,w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

For any
0<r<1
, define the integral operator

T_{r}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)F(rz,w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w),\quad z\in\mathbb{S}_{n}.

Then each
T_{r}
defines a bounded operator from
L^{2}(\lambda_{-1+2d})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
. Moreover,

\sup_{0<r<1}\|T_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\lambda_{-1+2d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})}<\infty.

Proof.

Take
p(w)=(1-|w|^{2})^{-d}
,
q(z)=1
. The integral kernel of
T_{r}
is

T_{r}(z,w):=\frac{B(n,2d)}{B(n,t+1)}F(rz,w)(1-|w|^{2})^{t+1-2d}.

As
t-d>-1
and
d<c
, by Lemma
2.3
, we have the following estimates of integrals,

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|T_{r}(z,w)|p(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{-1+2d}(w)\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{t-d}}{|1-\langle rz,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}}\mathrm{d}m(w)\lesssim 1=q(z),

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}|T_{r}(z,w)|q(z)\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma(z)}{\sigma_{2n-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{t+1-2d}}{|1-\langle z,rw\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}}\mathrm{d}\sigma(z)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1-|w|^{2})^{c-2d}\lesssim(1-|w|^{2})^{-d}=p(w).

The estimates are independent of
r
. Thus the conclusion follows from Schur’s test. This completes the proof of Lemma
3.2
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
t>-1
and
0<d<c<t+1
. Suppose
F(z,w)
is piecewise continuous on
\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{B}_{n}
, and

|F(z,w)|\leq\frac{1}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}},\quad\forall z,w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

Assume further that
F(z,w)
is holomorphic in
z
for each fixed
w
. Define the operator

Th(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)F(z,w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w),\quad z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

Then
T
defines a bounded operator from
L^{2}(\lambda_{-1+2d})
to
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
.

Proof.

Since
F(z,w)
is holomorphic in
z
, for every
h
, the function
Th
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{B}_{n}
. Denote

M=\sup_{0<r<1}\|T_{r}\|_{L^{2}(\lambda_{-1+2d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})}<\infty.

Then for any
h\in L^{2}(\lambda_{-1+2d})
, we verify the following identity

\sup_{0<r<1}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}|Th(rz)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\sigma(z)=\sup_{0<r<1}\|T_{r}h\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})}^{2}\leq M^{2}\|h\|_{L^{2}(\lambda_{-1+2d})}^{2}.

Therefore
T
is bounded. This completes the proof of Lemma
3.3
.
∎

The following fact about embedding operators between different weighted spaces is well-known to experts. In our proof, estimates of the operator norms and Schatten norms of these embeddings are needed. We give the calculation for completeness.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
t\geq-1
and
c>0
. Then for any
p>\frac{2n}{c}
the following hold.

(1)

The embedding map
E_{t,c}
from
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
to
L_{a,t+c}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
(identifying
L_{a,-1}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
with
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
) is in the Schatten
p
class
\mathcal{S}^{p}
.

(2)

There exists
C>0
, independent of
t
, such that

\|E_{t,c}\|=1,\quad\|E_{t,c}\|_{p}\leq C(t+n+1)^{\frac{n}{p}}.

Proof.

For any multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
, we have the norm

\|z^{\alpha}\|_{L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})}^{2}=\frac{\Gamma(n+t+1)\alpha!}{\Gamma(n+|\alpha|+t+1)}.

Thus
E_{t,c}
is unitarily equivalent to a diagonal operator with entry

\frac{\|z^{\alpha}\|_{L_{a,t+c}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})}}{\|z^{\alpha}\|_{L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})}}=\sqrt{\frac{\Gamma(n+t+c+1)\Gamma(n+|\alpha|+t+1)}{\Gamma(n+|\alpha|+t+c+1)\Gamma(n+t+1)}}=\sqrt{\frac{B(n+|\alpha|+t+1,c)}{B(n+t+1,c)}}

at
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
. It follows immediately that
\|E_{t,c}\|=1
. Thus it suffices to show

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}\bigg(\frac{B(n+|\alpha|+t+1,c)}{B(n+t+1,c)}\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}\frac{(d+n-1)!}{d!(n-1)!}\cdot\bigg(\frac{B(n+d+t+1,c)}{B(n+t+1,c)}\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\lesssim(t+n+1)^{n}.

Since
B(n+d+t+1,c)
is decreasing in
d
, and
\frac{(d+n-1)!}{d!}\approx d^{n-1}
for
d\geq 1
, we have the following inequalities,

\displaystyle\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}\frac{(d+n-1)!}{d!(n-1)!}\cdot\bigg(\frac{B(n+d+t+1,c)}{B(n+t+1,c)}\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 1+\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{n-1}\bigg(\frac{B(n+x+t+1,c)}{B(n+t+1,c)}\bigg)^{p/2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 1+\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{n-1}\bigg(\frac{n+t+1}{n+x+t+1}\bigg)^{\frac{pc}{2}}\mathrm{d}x.

If we take the change of variable
y=\frac{x}{n+t+1}
then the integral above has the following bounds,

\lesssim 1+(n+t+1)^{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{n-1}\bigg(\frac{1}{1+y}\bigg)^{\frac{pc}{2}}\mathrm{d}y\lesssim(n+t+1)^{n}

when
n<\frac{pc}{2}
, i.e.,
p>\frac{2n}{c}
. This completes the proof of Lemma
3.4
.
∎

4
Schatten Class Criteria

In this section, we obtain criteria for integral operators to belong to Schatten class. As explained in the beginning of Section
3
, the integral kernels may involve functions of
|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2}
. Thus we need to modify some of the well-known results to fit our case.

A standard way of proving Schatten class membership of integral operators is to use the following lemma. See
[
53
, Lemma 8.26]
for a proof when
n=1
, using interpolation. The exact same proof works for general
n
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose
t>-1
and
G(z,w)
is measurable on
\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{B}_{n}
. Suppose
p\geq 2
and

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}^{2}}|G(z,w)|^{p}|K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)<\infty.

Define the operator

Th(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)G(z,w)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w).

Then
T
defines a bounded operator on
L^{2}(\lambda_{t})
that belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{p}
.

Corollary 4.2
.

Suppose
t>-1
and
F(z,w)
is measurable on
\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{B}_{n}
. Suppose
c>0
and

|F(z,w)|\leq\frac{1}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

Define the integral operator

Th(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)F(z,w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w),\quad h\in L^{2}(\lambda_{t}).

Then
T
is bounded on
L^{2}(\lambda_{t})
, and for any
p>\frac{n}{c}
and
p\geq 2
,
T\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
.

Proof.

Let

G(z,w)=\frac{F(z,w)}{K^{(t)}_{w}(z)},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

Then by assumption, we have the following bound,

|G(z,w)|=|F(z,w)|\cdot|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t}\leq|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{c}.

Choose
p
so that
\frac{n}{c}<p<\frac{n+1+t}{c}
. Then by Lemma
2.3
, we compute the following integral.

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|G(z,w)|^{p}|K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{t}(1-|w|^{2})^{t}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{2(n+1+t)-cp}}\mathrm{d}m(w)\mathrm{d}m(z)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}(1-|z|^{2})^{t-(n+1+t-cp)}\mathrm{d}m(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}(1-|z|^{2})^{-1+(cp-n)}\mathrm{d}m(z)<\infty.

Since
G(z,w)
is bounded, for
p\geq\frac{n+1+t}{c}
the integral is also finite. Therefore we have the following bound,

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|G(z,w)|^{p}|K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)<\infty,\quad\forall p>\frac{n}{c}.

Finally, by Lemma
4.1
,
T\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
for any
p>\frac{n}{c}
and
p\geq 2
. This completes the proof of Corollary
4.2
.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
t,c,F,T
be as in Corollary
4.2
. Then for any Lipschitz function
u
on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
and any
p>\frac{n}{c+\frac{1}{2}}
and
p\geq 2
, the commutator

[T,M_{u}]\in\mathcal{S}^{p}.

Proof.

By definition, for
h\in L^{2}(\lambda_{t})
, we have the following expression,

[T,M_{u}]h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\big(u(w)-u(z)\big)F(z,w)h(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w).

Since
u
is Lipschitz, by Lemma
2.2
(6), we get the following bounds,

\big|\big(u(w)-u(z)\big)F(z,w)\big|\lesssim|z-w||F(z,w)|\lesssim\frac{1}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c-1/2}}.

The corollary then follows from Corollary
4.2
. This completes the proof of Corollary
4.3
.
∎

It is well-known that Hankel operators of Lipschitz symbols belong to
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for any
p>2n
. In fact, the Schatten-class membership for Hankel operators is completely characterized (see
[
38
]
,
[
53
, Theorem 8.36]
).

Corollary 4.4
.

Suppose
t>-1
and
u
is a Lipschitz function on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
. Then the Hankel operator
H^{(t)}_{u}=(I-P^{(t)})M_{u}P^{(t)}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for any
p>2n
.

Proof.

The corollary follows from Corollary
4.3
and the equation

H^{(t)}_{u}=(I-P^{(t)})M_{u}P^{(t)}=[M_{u},P^{(t)}]P^{(t)}.

This completes the proof of Corollary
4.4
.
∎

Corollaries
4.2
and
4.3
provide convenient criteria for an integral operator to be in
\mathcal{S}^{p}
, when
p\geq 2
. However, in this paper we will also need to deal with the case when
1\leq p<2
. Moreover, we will need to consider integral operators of the form

Th(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})F(z,w)h(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w),

where
\phi
is an unbounded function. For such
T
, if we take the double integral as in Lemma
4.1
, its integral is very likely to be infinite. In application, it is enough for us to obtain Schatten-class membership of the operators
TP^{(t)}
or
P^{(t)}T
. An alternative way is to take advantage of Lemma
3.4
. In particular, the following lemma holds.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose
t>-1
,
a>-n
,
b\geq 0
and
c>0
. Suppose
F(z,w)
is measurable on
\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{B}_{n}
,
\phi:(0,1)\to[0,\infty)
is measurable, and

\phi(s)\leq s^{a}(1-s)^{b},\quad s\in(0,1),

|F(z,w)|\leq\frac{1}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}},\quad\forall z,w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

Define the integral operator on
L^{2}(\lambda_{t})
.

Th(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})F(z,w)h(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w).

Then both
P^{(t)}T
and
TP^{(t)}
belong to
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for any
p>\max\{\frac{n}{c},\frac{n}{b+\frac{1+t}{2}}\}
.

Proof.

Notice that
P^{(t)}T\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
if and only if
T^{*}P^{(t)}\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
, and that
T^{*}
is an integral operator with integral kernel satisfying the same estimate as
T
. Thus it suffices to prove the statement for
TP^{(t)}
. For any
q>\max\{\frac{n}{c},\frac{n}{b+\frac{1+t}{2}}\}
, let
c^{\prime}=\frac{n}{q}
. Then
c^{\prime}<b+\frac{1+t}{2}
. Split the map
TP^{(t)}
as follows.

TP^{(t)}:L^{2}(\lambda_{t})\xrightarrow{P^{(t)}}L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\xrightarrow{E_{t,2c^{\prime}}}L_{a,t+2c^{\prime}}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\xrightarrow{\hat{T}}L^{2}(\lambda_{t}).

Here
\hat{T}:L_{a,t+2c^{\prime}}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\to L^{2}(\lambda_{t})
is defined by the same integral formula as
T
.
By Lemma
3.4
,
E_{t,2c^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
for any
p>\frac{n}{c^{\prime}}=q
. Also by Lemma
3.1
,
\hat{T}
is bounded. Since
q
is any number with
q>\max\{\frac{n}{c},\frac{n}{b+\frac{1+t}{2}}\}
, we have
TP^{(t)}\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\forall p>\max\{\frac{n}{c},\frac{n}{b+\frac{1+t}{2}}\}
. This completes the proof of Lemma
4.5
.
∎

In the case when
\phi=s^{k}\Phi^{(t)}_{n,k}
, the following Schatten-norm estimate holds.

Theorem 4.6
.

Suppose
t>-1
,
c>0
and
k
is a non-negative integer. Suppose
F(z,w)
is measurable on
\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{B}_{n}
, and

|F(z,w)|\leq\frac{|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2k}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}},\quad\forall z,w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

Define the integral operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n},\lambda_{t})
.

Th(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})F(z,w)h(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w).

Then both
P^{(t)}T
and
TP^{(t)}
belong to
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for any
p>\max\{\frac{n}{c},\frac{n}{k+\frac{1+t}{2}}\}
,
p\geq 1
. Moreover, for
p>\frac{n}{c}
, and
p\geq 1
, and
t
large enough, we have

\|P^{(t)}T\|_{p}\lesssim t^{-k+\frac{n}{p}},\quad\|TP^{(t)}\|_{p}\lesssim t^{-k+\frac{n}{p}}.

We postpone the proof of Theorem
4.6
to the end of this section. As in the proof of Corollary
4.3
, Lemma
4.5
and Theorem
4.6
imply the following.

Corollary 4.7
.

Suppose
t>-1
,
c>0
,
F(z,w)
is measurable on
\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{B}_{n}
, and

|F(z,w)|\leq\frac{1}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

Suppose
\phi:(0,1)\to[0,\infty)
is measurable. Define the integral operator on
L^{2}(\lambda_{t})
,

Th(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})F(z,w)h(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w).

Assume that
u
is a Lipschitz function on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
.

1.

Suppose
a>-n
,
b\geq 0
and
\phi(s)\leq s^{a}(1-s)^{b}
. Then

[T,M_{u}]P^{(t)},\quad P^{(t)}[T,M_{u}]\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\quad\forall p>\max\{\frac{n}{c+\frac{1}{2}},\frac{n}{b+\frac{1+t}{2}}\}.

2.

If
\phi(s)=s^{k}\Phi^{(t)}_{n,k}
, then for
t
large enough and
p>\frac{n}{c+\frac{1}{2}}
,

\big\|[T,M_{u}]P^{(t)}\big\|_{\mathcal{S}^{p}}\lesssim t^{-k+\frac{n}{p}},\quad\big\|P^{(t)}[T,M_{u}]\big\|_{\mathcal{S}^{p}}\lesssim t^{-k+\frac{n}{p}}.

A trivial application of Theorem
4.6
gives the following.

Lemma 4.8
.

Suppose
c>0
,
t>-1
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
satisfies

|f(z)|\leq(1-|z|^{2})^{c},\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

Then for any
p>\max\{\frac{n}{c},\frac{2n}{1+t}\},p\geq 1
,

T^{(t)}_{f}\in\mathcal{S}^{p}.

For any
p>\frac{n}{c}
,
p\geq 1
and
t
large enough,

\|T^{(t)}_{f}\|_{\mathcal{S}^{p}}\lesssim_{p}t^{\frac{n}{p}}.

Proof.

By definition, we have the following expression of
T^{(t)}_{f}
,

T^{(t)}_{f}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}f(w)h(w)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w).

By the assumption, we have the following inequalities

|f(w)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|\leq\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{c}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t}}\lesssim\frac{1}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}}.

Since
\Phi^{(t)}_{n,0}=1
, the conclusion follows directly from Theorem
4.6
. This completes the proof of Lemma
4.8
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose
\phi:(0,1)\to[0,\infty)
is measurable. Then for any
c,d\in\mathbb{R}
there exist
C>0
and
t_{0}>0
such that whenever
t>t_{0}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{\frac{t}{2}+c}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t+d}}\mathrm{d}m(w)\leq C\int_{0}^{1}\phi(s)s^{n-1}(1-s)^{\frac{t}{4}}\mathrm{d}s\cdot(1-|z|^{2})^{-\frac{t}{2}-d+c},\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{B}_{n}.

(4.1)

Proof.

Make the change of variable
w=\varphi_{z}(\xi)
. Using Lemma
2.2
we have the following equation,

\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{t}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t}}\mathrm{d}m(w){\mathrel{\to}^{w=\varphi_{z}(\xi)}_{\xi=\varphi_{z}(w)}}\frac{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{t}}{|1-\langle z,\xi\rangle|^{n+1+t}}\mathrm{d}m(\xi).

Then we have the following estimate of the left side of Equation (
4.1
),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{\frac{t}{2}+c}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t+d}}\mathrm{d}m(w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|\xi|^{2})\bigg(\frac{(1-|z|^{2})(1-|\xi|^{2})}{|1-\langle z,\xi\rangle|^{2}}\bigg)^{c-\frac{t}{2}}\bigg(\frac{|1-\langle z,\xi\rangle|}{1-|z|^{2}}\bigg)^{d}\frac{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{t}}{|1-\langle z,\xi\rangle|^{n+1+t}}\mathrm{d}m(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-|z|^{2})^{c-\frac{t}{2}-d}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|\xi|^{2})\frac{(1-|\xi|^{2})^{c+\frac{t}{2}}}{|1-\langle z,\xi\rangle|^{n+1+2c-d}}\mathrm{d}m(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-|z|^{2})^{c-\frac{t}{2}-d}\int_{0}^{1}\phi(r^{2})(1-r^{2})^{c+\frac{t}{2}}r^{2n-1}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}\frac{1}{|1-\langle rz,\zeta\rangle|^{n+1+2c-d}}\mathrm{d}\sigma(\zeta)\bigg]\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1-|z|^{2})^{c-\frac{t}{2}-d}\int_{0}^{1}\phi(r^{2})(1-r^{2})^{c+\frac{t}{2}}r^{2n-1}(1-|rz|^{2})^{-a}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1-|z|^{2})^{c-\frac{t}{2}-d}\int_{0}^{1}\phi(r^{2})(1-r^{2})^{c-a+\frac{t}{2}}r^{2n-1}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\to}^{s=r^{2}}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(1-|z|^{2})^{c-\frac{t}{2}-d}\int_{0}^{1}\phi(s)(1-s)^{c-a+\frac{t}{2}}s^{n-1}\mathrm{d}s.

Here
a=2c-d
if
2c-d>0
;
a=\frac{1}{2}
if
2c-d=0
;
a=0
if
2c-d<0
. For
t
large enough,
c-a+\frac{t}{2}>\frac{t}{4}
. So we have the following inequality,

\int_{0}^{1}\phi(s)(1-s)^{c-a+\frac{t}{2}}s^{n-1}\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{0}^{1}\phi(s)(1-s)^{\frac{t}{4}}s^{n-1}\mathrm{d}s.

This completes the proof of Lemma
4.9
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

The Schatten class memberships of
TP^{(t)}
and
P^{(t)}T
are implied by Lemma
4.5
and
[
47
, Lemma 8.3]
. It remains to prove the Schatten norm estimates. We may assume that
t
is large enough so that
0<c<k+\frac{1+t}{2}
.
Also, as in the proof of Lemma
4.5
, it suffices to prove the statement for
TP^{(t)}
.

Split the operator
TP^{(t)}
as

L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n},\lambda_{t})\xrightarrow{P^{(t)}}L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\xrightarrow{E_{t,2c}}L_{a,t+2c}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\xrightarrow{\hat{T}}L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n},\lambda_{t}),

where
\hat{T}
is the same integral operator as
T
.
By Lemma
3.4
, it suffices to show that
\hat{T}
defines a bounded operator from
L_{a,t+2c}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n},\lambda_{t})
with
\|\hat{T}\|_{L_{a,t+2c}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\to L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})}\lesssim t^{-k}
for large
t
.

This is done by Schur’s test. Since
c<k+\frac{t+1}{2}
, if we take
x=c+\frac{t+1}{2}
, then
t+1+k>x>2c-k
. Take
p(w)=(1-|w|^{2})^{-x}
and
q(z)=(1-|z|^{2})^{c-x}
. The integral kernel of
\hat{T}:L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n},\lambda_{t+2c})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n},\lambda_{t})
is

T(z,w):=\frac{B(n,t+2c+1)}{B(n,t+1)}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})F(z,w)(1-|w|^{2})^{-2c}.

Then by (
2.6
), (
4.1
), (
2.19
) and (
2.20
), we have the following estimates,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|T(z,w)|p(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t+2c}(w)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B(n,t+1)}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\Phi^{(t)}_{n,k}(|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2})|\varphi_{z}(w)|^{2k}\frac{(1-|w|^{2})^{t-x}}{|1-\langle z,w\rangle|^{n+1+t-c}}\mathrm{d}m(w)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{0}^{1}\Phi_{n,k}^{(t)}(s)s^{n+k-1}(1-s)^{\frac{t}{4}}\mathrm{d}s}{B(n,t+1)}q(z)\lesssim t^{-k}q(z).

Similarly, we have the following inequality,

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|T(z,w)|q(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(z)\lesssim t^{-k}p(w).

From this we have the following bound for
p>n/c
and large
t
,

\|T\|_{\mathcal{S}^{p}}\leq\|E_{t,2c}\|_{\mathcal{S}^{p}}\|\hat{T}\|\lesssim t^{-k+n/p}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
4.6
.
∎

5
Traces on Different Weighted Bergman Spaces

As explained in the introduction, the goal of this section is to prove Equation (
1.8
). More precisely, we will prove Lemma
5.1
and Lemma
5.2
stated below.

Suppose
t>s\geq-1
. It is well-known that
L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\subset L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
, and
L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
is dense in
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
. For a Toeplitz operator
T^{(t)}_{f}
on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
, if the restriction of
T^{(t)}_{f}
on
L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
defines a bounded operator on
L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
, then we denote this restriction to be
T^{(t,s)}_{f}
. On the other hand, if a Toeplitz operator
T^{(s)}_{f}
on
L_{a,s}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
extends (uniquely) into a bounded operator on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
, then we denote this operator to be
T^{(s,t)}_{f}
. It follows from Lemma
3.1
that if
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
and
t>s>-1
, then the restricted operator
T^{(t,s)}_{f}
is well-defined.

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{2n}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
and
t>-1
. Then

[T^{(t)}_{f_{1}},T^{(t)}_{f_{2}},\ldots,T^{(t)}_{f_{2n}}]-[T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{1}},T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{2}},\ldots,T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{2n}}]

is a trace class operator on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
with zero trace.

In the case of the Hardy space we show the following.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{2n}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
. Then

[T_{f_{1}},T_{f_{2}},\ldots,T_{f_{2n}}]-[T^{(1,-1)}_{f_{1}},T^{(1,-1)}_{f_{2}},\ldots,T^{(1,-1)}_{f_{2n}}]

is a trace class operator on
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
with zero trace.

The fact that each
T^{(1,-1)}_{f_{i}}
is well-defined will be explained in Remark
5.21
. The proof of Lemma
5.1
(and Lemma
5.2
) involves writing
T^{(t)}_{f}
(resp.
T_{f}
) as the sum of
T^{(t+1,t)}_{f}
(resp.
T^{(1,-1)}_{f}
) and some perturbation operators.

Definition 5.3
.

For
t>-1
, define

X^{(t)}_{w}(z)=\frac{t+1}{n+t+1}\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n}1(|w|^{2})\bar{R}_{w}K^{(t)}_{w}(z)-K^{(t+1)}_{w}(z),

(5.1)

and

Y^{(t)}_{w}(z)=\frac{t+1}{n+t+1}\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n}1(|w|^{2})K^{(t)}_{w}(z).

(5.2)

For a symbol function
g
, formally define the integral operators

X^{(t)}_{g}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}g(w)h(w)X^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t+1}(w);\quad Y^{(t)}_{g}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}g(w)h(w)Y^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t+1}(w).

It will be clear in subsequent proof that for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
,
X^{(t)}_{f}
and
Y^{(t)}_{\bar{R}f}
define bounded operators on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
(see Lemma
5.16
and Lemma
5.14
).

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose
f\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
. Then for any
t>-1
,

T^{(t)}_{f}=T^{(t+1,t)}_{f}+X^{(t)}_{f}+Y^{(t)}_{\bar{R}f}+f(0)E_{0},

(5.3)

where
E_{0}h=h(0)
is a rank one operator.

Proof.

For any
h\in\mathrm{Hol}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
, by Lemma
2.11
, we compute
T^{(t)}_{f}
as follows,

\displaystyle T^{(t)}_{f}h(z)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}f(w)h(w)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)h(0)+\frac{t+1}{n+t+1}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n}1(|w|^{2})\bar{R}f(w)h(w)K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t+1}(w)

\displaystyle+\frac{t+1}{n+t+1}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}^{(t)}_{n}1(|w|^{2})f(w)h(w)\bar{R}K^{(t)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t+1}(w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)\big(E_{0}f\big)(z)+Y^{(t)}_{\bar{R}f}h(z)+X^{(t)}_{f}h(z)+T^{(t+1,t)}_{f}h(z).

This completes the proof of Lemma
5.4
.
∎

In the case of the Hardy space, we need to apply integration by parts twice to make our arguments work.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
\phi(s)=s^{-n}
.
Define

X^{(-1)}_{w}(z)=\frac{1}{n(n+1)}\mathcal{G}_{n}^{(0)}\phi(|w|^{2})\bar{R}^{2}_{w}K_{w}(z)-K_{w}^{(1)}(z),

Y^{(-1)}_{w}(z)=\frac{2}{n(n+1)}\mathcal{G}_{n}^{(0)}\phi(|w|^{2})\bar{R}_{w}K_{w}(z),

and

Z^{(-1)}_{w}(z)=\frac{1}{n(n+1)}\mathcal{G}_{n}^{(0)}\phi(|w|^{2})K_{w}(z).

Formally define the symboled integral operators

X^{(-1)}_{f}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)f(w)X^{(-1)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}(w),

Y^{(-1)}_{f}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)f(w)Y^{(-1)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}(w),

and

Z^{(-1)}_{f}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)f(w)Z^{(-1)}_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}(w).

Again, it will be clear later that for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
,
X^{(-1)}_{f}
,
Y^{(-1)}_{\bar{R}f}
and
Z^{(-1)}_{\bar{R}^{2}f}
define bounded operators on
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}_{n})
(see Lemma
5.20
).

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose
f\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
. Then

T_{f}=T^{(1,-1)}_{f}+X^{(-1)}_{f}+Y^{(-1)}_{\bar{R}f}+Z^{(-1)}_{\bar{R}^{2}f}+f(0)E_{0}.

Taking
\alpha=\beta=0
and
z=0
in Lemma
2.12
, we get the following.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose
v\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
. Then

\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}v(w)\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma(w)}{\sigma_{2n-1}}=v(0)+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}|w|^{-2n}\bar{R}v(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{0}(w).

(5.4)

Also, taking
\alpha=\beta=0,t=0,z=0
in Lemma
2.10
gives the following.

Lemma 5.8
.

Suppose
\phi:(0,1)\to[0,\infty)
is measurable and
v\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
. Then whenever all integrals converge absolutely, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|w|^{2})v(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{0}(w)

(5.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{\mathcal{F}^{(0)}_{n}\phi(0)}{B(n,t+1)}v(0)+\frac{1}{n+1}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}^{(0)}_{n}\phi(|w|^{2})\bar{R}v(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}(w),&\mathcal{F}^{(0)}_{n}\phi(0)<\infty\\
\frac{1}{n+1}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}^{(0)}_{n}\phi(|w|^{2})\bar{R}v(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}(w),&\mathcal{F}^{(0)}_{n}\phi(0)\leq\infty,~v(0)=0\end{cases}.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

By Lemma
5.7
and Lemma
5.8
, for
h\in\mathrm{Hol}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
, we compute
T_{f}
as follows,

\displaystyle T_{f}h(z)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}h(w)f(w)K_{w}(z)\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma(w)}{\sigma_{2n-1}}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\to}^{\eqref{eqn: formula Sn R 0}}}

\displaystyle f(0)h(0)+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\phi(|w|^{2})\bar{R}\bigg[h(w)f(w)K_{w}(z)\bigg]\mathrm{d}\lambda_{0}(w)

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\to}^{\eqref{eqn: formula Bn R 0}}}

\displaystyle f(0)h(0)+\frac{1}{n(n+1)}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}_{n}^{(0)}\phi(|w|^{2})\bar{R}^{2}\bigg[h(w)f(w)K_{w}(z)\bigg]\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}(w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)h(0)+\frac{1}{n(n+1)}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}\mathcal{G}_{n}^{(0)}\phi(|w|^{2})h(w)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}(w)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\bigg[\bar{R}^{2}f(w)K_{w}(z)+2\bar{R}f(w)\bar{R}_{w}K_{w}(z)+f(w)\bar{R}_{w}^{2}K_{w}(z)\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)\big(E_{0}f\big)(z)+T^{(1,-1)}_{f}h(z)+X^{(-1)}_{f}h(z)+Y^{(-1)}_{\bar{R}f}h(z)+Z^{(-1)}_{\bar{R}^{2}f}h(z).

This completes the proof of Lemma
5.6
.
∎

In view of (
5.3
), Lemma
5.1
essentially says that the trace

\mathrm{Tr}[T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{1}},T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{2}},\ldots,T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{2n}}]

if exist, is invariant under the perturbations of
X^{(t)}_{f_{i}}
,
Y^{(t)}_{\bar{R}f_{i}}
and
f_{i}(0)E_{0}
. Similarly, Lemma
5.2
can also be interpreted as the stability of trace under certain perturbations.

5.1
Hypotheses A

In this subsection, let us assume the following.

Hypotheses A:
Suppose
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n},B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{2n}
are bounded linear operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Denote
C_{i}=A_{i}+B_{i},i=1,\ldots,2n
. The operators satisfy the following properties.

(1)

For any
i=1,\ldots,2n
,

B_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\quad\forall p>n.

(2)

For any
i,j=1,\ldots,2n
,

[A_{i},A_{j}]\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\quad\forall p>n.

(3)

For any
i,j=1,\ldots,2n,

[A_{i},B_{j}]\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\quad\text{ for some }p<n.

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following.

Proposition 5.9
.

Assume Hypotheses A. Then the operator

[C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{2n}]-[A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n}]

is in the trace class. Moreover, the operator

[C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{2n}]-[A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n}]-\sum_{k=1}^{2n}[A_{1},\ldots,A_{k-1},B_{k},A_{k+1},\ldots,A_{2n}]

is a trace class operator with zero trace.

Lemma 5.10
.

Suppose
\{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}\}
is a subset of
\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{2n},B_{1},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
, and at least two of
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}
are in
\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
. Then the operator

[X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}]

is in the trace class of zero trace.

Proof.

The lemma can be restated as follows:

“if
B\in\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
,
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n-1}\}\subset\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{2n},B_{1},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
, and at least one of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n-1}
is in
\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
, then the operator

[B,X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n-1}]

is in the trace class with zero trace.”

Under the above assumption on
B
and
X_{1},\cdots,X_{2n-1}
, we compute the antisymmetrization
[B,X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n-1}]
as follows.

\displaystyle[B,X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n-1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigg(\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)X_{\tau_{1}}\ldots X_{\tau_{2k-2}}BX_{\tau_{2k-1}}\ldots X_{\tau_{2n-1}}-\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)X_{\tau_{1}}\ldots X_{\tau_{2k-1}}BX_{\tau_{2k}}\ldots X_{\tau_{2n-1}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigg(\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots X_{\tau_{2k-2}}BX_{\tau_{2k-1}}\ldots X_{\tau_{2n-1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots X_{\tau_{2k-1}}BX_{\tau_{2k}}\ldots X_{\tau_{2n-1}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigg(\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]BX_{\tau_{2k-1}}[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}]

\displaystyle~~~~-\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]X_{\tau_{2k-1}}B[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}]\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)\bigg([X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]BX_{\tau_{2k-1}}[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}]

\displaystyle~~~~-[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]X_{\tau_{2k-1}}B[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}]\bigg).

In this proof, we use the following notation.

Notation 5.11
.

For operators
S,T
, write
S\sim T
when
S-T
is a trace class operator with zero trace.

For each
k=1,\ldots,n
and
\tau\in S_{2n-1}
, we
claim
that

\displaystyle[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]BX_{\tau_{2k-1}}[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}]

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle BX_{\tau_{2k-1}}[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}][X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}],

and

\displaystyle[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]X_{\tau_{2k-1}}B[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}]

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle B[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}][X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]X_{\tau_{2k-1}}.

If
X_{\tau_{2k-1}}\in\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
, then we have

B,X_{\tau_{2k-1}}\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\forall p>n,\quad[X_{\tau_{i}},X_{\tau_{j}}]\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\forall p>n,\forall i,j=1,\ldots,2n-1.

By Remark
2.7
it is easy to see that the claim holds. If
X_{\tau_{2k-1}}\in\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{2n}\}
, then at least one of
X_{\tau_{1}},\ldots,X_{\tau_{2k-2}},X_{\tau_{2k}},\ldots,X_{\tau_{2n-1}}
is in
\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
. Thus at least one of the commutators

[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}],\ldots,[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}],[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}],\ldots,[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}]

is in
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for some
p<n
. Again, by Remark
2.7
, the claim also follows. Thus in both cases the claim holds. By the claim, we compute
[B,X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n-1}]
.

\displaystyle[B,X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n-1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)\bigg([X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]BX_{\tau_{2k-1}}[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}]

\displaystyle~~~~-[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]X_{\tau_{2k-1}}B[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}]\bigg)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle 2^{-n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)BX_{\tau_{2k-1}}[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}][X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]

\displaystyle-2^{-n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)B[X_{\tau_{2k}},X_{\tau_{2k+1}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}][X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2k-3}},X_{\tau_{2k-2}}]X_{\tau_{2k-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)BX_{\tau_{1}}[X_{\tau_{2}},X_{\tau_{3}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-2}},X_{\tau_{2n-1}}]

\displaystyle-2^{-n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)B[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-3}},X_{\tau_{2n-2}}]X_{\tau_{2n-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)BX_{\tau_{1}}\ldots X_{\tau_{2n-1}}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)BX_{\tau_{1}}\ldots X_{\tau_{2n-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Here the third-to-last equality is because the antisymmetric sums
\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n-1}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)\cdots
are invariant under any even permutation.
This completes the proof of Lemma
5.10
.
∎

A verbatim repetition of the proof of Lemma
5.10
also proves the following.

Corollary 5.12
.

Assume in addition to Hypotheses A that

B_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\quad\text{ for some}~p<n.

Suppose
\{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}\}\subset\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{2n},B_{1},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
, and at least one of
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}\in\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
. Then the operator

[X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}]

is in the trace class with zero trace. Consequently, the operator

[C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{2n}]-[A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n}]

is in the trace class with zero trace.

Remark 5.13
.

Notice that the assumption of Corollary
5.12
is equivalent to the following.

1.

For
i,j=1,\ldots,2n
,

[A_{i},A_{j}]\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\quad\forall p>n.

2.

For
i=1,\ldots,2n
,

B_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{p},\quad\text{ for some }p<n.

Thus Corollary
5.12
essentially says that the trace

\mathrm{Tr}[A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n}]

is stable under any perturbation that belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for some
p<n
.

Proof of Proposition
5.9
.

By definition, we have the following equation

[C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{2n}]-[A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n}]=\sum[X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}],

where the sum is taken over all tuples
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n})
such that each
X_{i}
belongs to
\{A_{i},B_{i}\}
, and at least one of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{2n}
belongs to
\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
. For this tuple
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n})
, we have the following expansion

\displaystyle[X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)X_{\tau_{1}}X_{\tau_{2}}\ldots X_{\tau_{2n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-n}\sum_{\tau\in S_{2n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}][X_{\tau_{3}},X_{\tau_{4}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-1}},X_{\tau_{2n}}].

By Hypotheses A, each commutator
[X_{i},X_{j}]
is at least in
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for any
p>n
, and when
X_{i}\in\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
,
[X_{i},X_{j}]\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
for some
p<n
,
\forall j=1,\ldots,2n
. Thus by Lemma
2.5
, each product

[X_{\tau_{1}},X_{\tau_{2}}][X_{\tau_{3}},X_{\tau_{4}}]\ldots[X_{\tau_{2n-1}},X_{\tau_{2n}}]

is in the trace class. So we obtain the following estimate

[C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{2n}]-[A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n}]\in\mathcal{S}^{1}.

On the other hand, we have the following equation

\displaystyle[C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{2n}]-[A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n}]-\sum_{k=1}^{2n}[A_{1},\ldots,A_{k-1},B_{k},A_{k+1},\ldots,A_{2n}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum[X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}],

where each
X_{i}\in\{A_{i},B_{i}\}
, and at least two of
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}
is in
\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{2n}\}
. By Lemma
5.10
, each
[X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2n}]
is a trace class operator of zero trace. Thus we conclude that

[C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{2n}]-[A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n}]-\sum_{k=1}^{2n}[A_{1},\ldots,A_{k-1},B_{k},A_{k+1},\ldots,A_{2n}]

is a trace class operator of zero trace. This completes the proof of Proposition
5.9
.
∎

5.2
Proof of Lemmas
5.1
and
5.2

In this subsection, temporarily fix the notations

A_{i}=\begin{cases}T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{i}},&\text{ if }t>-1\\
T^{(1,-1)}_{f_{i}},&\text{ if }t=-1\end{cases},\quad B_{i}=\begin{cases}X^{(t)}_{f_{i}}+Y^{(t)}_{\bar{R}f_{i}}+f_{i}(0)E_{0},&\text{ if }t>-1\\
X^{(-1)}_{f_{i}}+Y^{(-1)}_{\bar{R}f_{i}}+Z^{(-1)}_{\bar{R}^{2}f_{i}}+f_{i}(0)E_{0},&\text{ if }t=-1\end{cases},

and
C_{i}=A_{i}+B_{i}
. Then by Lemma
5.4
and Lemma
5.6
,

C_{i}=\begin{cases}T^{(t)}_{f_{i}},&\text{ if }t>-1,\\
T_{f_{i}},&\text{ if }t=-1.\end{cases}

Thus Lemmas
5.1
and
5.2
are equivalent to the property that

[C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{2n}]-[A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{2n}]

is a trace class operator with zero trace.

Suppose
X_{w}(z),Y_{w}(z)
are measurable functions on
\mathbb{B}_{n}\times\mathbb{B}_{n}
. We introduce the following hypotheses.

For
t>-1
, we say that
X_{w}(z),Y_{w}(z)
satisfy
Hypotheses B at
t
, if there are
\epsilon>0
and
C>0
such that the following hold.

1.

For each
w\in\mathbb{B}_{n}
,
X_{w}(z),Y_{w}(z)
is holomorphic in
z
,

2.

|X_{w}(z)|\leq C|w|^{-2n+\epsilon}|K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|
,

3.

|Y_{w}(z)|\leq C|w|^{-2n-1+\epsilon}|K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|
.

For a function
f
on
\mathbb{B}_{n}
, formally define the symboled integral operators

X_{f}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)f(w)X_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t+1}(w),\quad Y_{f}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)f(w)Y_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t+1}(w).

(5.6)

Lemma 5.14
.

Assume Hypotheses B. Then for any
f\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
, the integral operators
X_{f}
and
Y_{\bar{R}f}
define bounded operators on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
that belong to
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for any
p>n
.

Proof.

For
f\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
, we have

|f(w)|\lesssim 1,\quad|\bar{R}f(w)|\lesssim|w|.

Thus by Hypotheses B, we obtain the following estimates,

|f(w)X_{w}(z)|\lesssim|w|^{-2n+\epsilon}|K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|,\quad|\bar{R}f(w)Y_{w}(z)|\lesssim|w|^{-2n+\epsilon}|K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|.

Thus for any
h\in\mathrm{Hol}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
,
X_{f}h(z),Y_{\bar{R}f}h(z)
are defined pointwise. If
f
has compact support contained in
\mathbb{B}_{n}
, then it is easy to see that
X_{f},Y_{\bar{R}f}
belong to
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for any
p
. In general, we can always write
f=f_{1}+f_{2}
, where
f_{1}
has compact support in
\mathbb{B}_{n}
, and the support of
f_{2}
is away from the origin. We might as well assume that
f
itself has support away from zero. In this case, we have the following estimates

|f(w)X_{w}(z)|\lesssim|K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|,\quad|\bar{R}f(w)Y_{w}(z)|\lesssim|K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|.

Denote
G_{w}(z)
to be either
f(w)X_{w}(z)
or
\bar{R}f(w)Y_{w}(z)
. Then
G_{w}(z)
is holomorphic in
z
, and

|G_{w}(z)|\lesssim|K^{(t)}_{w}(z)|.

Define

T_{G}h(z)=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{n}}h(w)G_{w}(z)\mathrm{d}\lambda_{t+1}(w).

Then
T_{G}
equals either
X_{f}
or
Y_{\bar{R}f}
. Split the map as follows.

T_{G}:L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\xrightarrow{E_{t,2}}L_{a,t+2}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\xrightarrow{\hat{T}_{G}}L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n}),

where
\hat{T}_{G}
is defined by the same integral formula as
T_{G}
. By Lemma
3.1
,
\hat{T}_{G}
is bounded. By Lemma
3.4
,
E_{t,2}\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
for all
p>n
. Therefore
T_{G}\in\mathcal{S}^{p}
for all
p>n
. This completes the proof of Lemma
5.14
.
∎

We postpone the proof of the following proposition to the end of this section.

Proposition 5.15
.

Suppose
t>-1
and
X_{w}(z),Y_{w}(z)
satisfy Hypotheses B at
t
. Suppose that
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{2n}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathbb{B}_{n}})
, and
K_{1},K_{2},\ldots,K_{2n}
are bounded operators on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
that belong to
\mathcal{S}^{p}
for some
p<n
. Denote

\hat{A}_{i}=T^{(t+1,t)}_{f_{i}},\quad\hat{B}_{i}=X_{f_{i}}+Y_{\bar{R}f_{i}}+K_{i},\quad\hat{C}_{i}=\hat{A}_{i}+\hat{B}_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,2n.

Then the operator

[\hat{C}_{1},\hat{C}_{2},\ldots,\hat{C}_{2n}]-[\hat{A}_{1},\hat{A}_{2},\ldots,\hat{A}_{2n}]

is a trace class operator with zero trace on
L_{a,t}^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{n})
.

Lemma 5.16
.

For
t>-1
, the integral kernels
X^{(t)}_{w}(z)
and
Y^{(t)}_{w}(z)
satisfy Hypotheses B at
t
.

Proof.

The fact that
X^{(t)}_{w}(z)
and
Y^{(t)}_{w}(z)
are holomorphic in
z
is obvious from Definition
5.3
.
